On Estimation and Inference of Large Approximate Dynamic Factor Models via Principal Component Analysis


Abstract
We provide an alternative derivation of the asymptotic results for the Principal Components estimator of a large approximate factor model. Results are derived under a minimal set of assumptions and, in particular, we require only the existence of 4th order moments. A special focus is given to the time series setting, a case considered in almost all recent econometric applications of factor models. Hence, estimation is based on the classical n × n n\times n sample covariance matrix and not on a T × T T\times T covariance matrix often considered in the literature. Indeed, despite the two approaches being asymptotically equivalent, the former is more coherent with a time series setting and it immediately allows us to write more intuitive asymptotic expansions for the Principal Component estimators showing that they are equivalent to OLS as long as n / T → 0 \sqrt{n}/T\to 0 and T / n → 0 \sqrt{T}/n\to 0 , that is the loadings are estimated in a time series regression as if the factors were known, while the factors are estimated in a cross-sectional regression as if the loadings were known. Finally, we give some alternative sets of primitive sufficient conditions for mean-squared consistency of the sample covariance matrix of the factors, of the idiosyncratic components, and of the observed time series, which is the starting point for Principal Component Analysis. Keywords: Approximate Dynamic Factor Model; Principal Component Analysis; Inference



Matteo Barigozzi
†

1
Model and Assumptions

We adopt the following approach taken from time series analysis. Frist, we define the model for an infinite dimensional process
\{x_{it},\,i\in\mathbb{N},\,t\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Second, the properties and the assumptions of the model are defined for the
n
-dimensional sub-process
\{x_{it},\,i=1\ldots,n,\,t\in\mathbb{Z}\}
in the limit
n\to\infty
. Third, the properties of the estimators are derived for a given
nT
-dimensional realization
\{x_{it},\,i=1\ldots,n,\,t=1,\ldots,T\}
in the limit
n,T\to\infty
.

We define a factor model driven by
r
factors as

\displaystyle x_{it}

\displaystyle=\bm{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t}+\xi_{it},\hskip 10.00002pti\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{Z}.

(1)

where
\bm{\lambda}_{i}=(\lambda_{i1}\cdots\lambda_{ir})^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{F}_{t}=(F_{1t}\cdots F_{rt})^{\prime}
are the
r
-dimensional vectors of loadings for series
i
and factors, respectively. We call
\xi_{it}
the idiosyncratic component and

\chi_{it}=\bm{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\mathbf{F}_{t}
the common component.

Model (
1
) in vector notation reads

\mathbf{x}_{t}=\bm{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}+\bm{\xi}_{t},\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{Z},

where
\mathbf{x}_{t}=(x_{1t}\cdots x_{nt})^{\prime}
and
\bm{\xi}_{t}=(\xi_{1t}\cdots\xi_{nt})^{\prime}
are
n
-dimensional vectors of observables and idiosyncratic components, respectively, and
\bm{\Lambda}=(\bm{\lambda}_{1}\cdots\bm{\lambda}_{n})^{\prime}
is the
n\times r
matrix of factor loadings. We call
\bm{\chi}_{t}=\bm{\Lambda}\mathbf{F}_{t}
the vector of common components.

Given an observed sample of
T
observations, then we can write model (
1
) also in matrix notation as

\bm{X}=\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}+\bm{\Xi},

where
\bm{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{x}_{T})^{\prime}
and
\bm{\Xi}=(\bm{\xi}_{1}\cdots\bm{\xi}_{T})^{\prime}
are
T\times n
matrices of observables and idiosyncratic components, and
\bm{F}=(\mathbf{F}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{F}_{T})^{\prime}
is the
T\times r
matrix of factors. This latter notation is used for estimation mainly but not for describing the model in population where we deal with stochastic processes and so
t\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We characterize the common component by means of the following assumption.

Assumption 1

(
common component
)
.

(a)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\|n^{-1}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}-\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}\|=0
, where
\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}
is
r\times r
positive definite, and, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\|\bm{\lambda}_{i}\|\leq M_{\Lambda}
for some finite positive real
M_{\Lambda}
independent of
i
.

(b)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{F}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{r}
and
\bm{\Gamma}^{F}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}]
is
r\times r
positive definite and
\|\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\|\leq M_{F}
for some finite positive real
M_{F}
independent of
t
.

(c)

(i)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{F}_{t}\|^{4}]\leq K_{F}
for some finite positive real
K_{F}
independent of
t
;

(ii)

\mathrm{p}\text{-}\lim_{T\to\infty}\left\|T^{-1}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}-\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\right\|=0
.

(d)

There exists an integer
N
such that for all
n>N
,
r
is a finite positive integer, independent of
n
.

Parts (a) and (b), are also assumed in
Bai 2003
for PC estimation. They imply that, the loadings matrix has asymptotically maximum column rank
r
(part (a)) and the factors have a finite full-rank covariance matrix (part (b)). Moreover, because of part (a), for any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, all the factors have a finite contribution to each series (upper bound on
\|\bm{\lambda}_{i}\|
). Because of Assumption
1
(a) we have that the eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}
, denoted as
\mu_{j}(n^{-1}{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}})
,
j=1,\ldots,r
are such that (see also Lemma
3
(iv))

m_{\Lambda}^{2}\leq\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\leq M_{\Lambda}^{2},

for some finite positive real
m_{\Lambda}
independent of
n
and where also
M_{\Lambda}
is independent of
n
. Therefore, the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}
, denoted as
\mu_{j}(\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda})
,
j=1,\ldots,r
are such that:

m_{\Lambda}^{2}\leq\mu_{j}(\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda})\leq M_{\Lambda}^{2}.

(2)

Similarly, because of Assumption
1
(b) we have that the eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}
, denoted as
\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)
,
j=1,\ldots,r
are such that (this is because of stationarity, see also Lemma
3
(v))

m_{F}\leq\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)\leq M_{F},

for some finite positive real
m_{F}
independent of
T
and where also
M_{F}
is independent of
T
. Therefore, because of Assumption
1
(c), the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Gamma}^{F}
, denoted as
\mu_{j}(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})
,
j=1,\ldots,r
are such that:

m_{F}\leq\mu_{j}(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})\leq M_{F}.

(3)

Part (c) is made also in
Bai 2003
. In part (c-i) we assume finite 4th order moments of the factors, indeed

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{F}_{t}\|^{4}]=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{r}\mathbb{E}[F_{jt}^{2}F_{kt}^{2}].

Part (c-ii) is very general, it simply says that the sample covariance matrix of the factors is a consistent estimator of its population counterpart
\bm{\Gamma}^{F}
. In principle, we do not have to require for stationarity in part (b) and we can have
\bm{\Gamma}^{F}
that depends on
t
as long as (
3
) still holds. For this reason we stress that
M_{F}
in part (b) is independent of
t
. This is, for example the case when we are in presence of structural breaks/change-points (see, e.g.,
Barigozzi
et al. 2018
and
Duan
et al. 2022
) or regime shifts (
Massacci 2017
;
Barigozzi and
Massacci 2022
). Unit roots are however excluded since in that case
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
does not have a finite covariance, and everything that is done in this paper would have to be applied to properly differenced data. Still the assumption requires introducing the sample size
T
and does not provide a rate, which should be
\sqrt{T}
. If we assume stationarity, then, a discussion on the primitive conditions the process
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
needs to satisfy is given in Section
3
where assumptions alternative to part (c) are proposed.

We only consider non-random factor loadings for simplicity. As in
Bai 2003
, the generalization to the case of random loadings is possible provided that: we assume convergence in probability in part (a), for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\lambda}_{i}\|^{4}]\leq K_{\Lambda}
, for some finite positive real
K_{\Lambda}
independent of
i
, and
\{\bm{\lambda}_{i},\,i\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an independent sequence.

Part (d) implies the existence of a finite number of factors. In particular, the numbers of common factors,
r
, is identified only for
n\to\infty
. Here
N
is the minimum number of series we need to be able to identify
r
so that
r\leq N
. Without loss of generality hereafter when we say “for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
” we always mean that
n>N
so that
r
can be identified. In practice we must always work with
n
such that
r<n
. Moreover, because PCA is based on eigenvalues of a matrix
n\times n
estimated using
T
observations then we must also have samples of size
T
such that
r<T
.
Therefore, sometimes it is directly assumed that
r<\min(n,T)
.

Let
\bm{\Gamma}^{\chi}=\mathbb{E}[\bm{\chi}_{t}\bm{\chi}_{t}^{\prime}]=\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
with
r
largest eigenvalues
\mu_{j}^{\chi}
,
j=1,\ldots,r
, collected in the
r\times r
diagonal matrix
{\mathbf{M}}^{\chi}
(sorted in descending order) and with corresponding normalized eigenvectors which are the columns of the
n\times r
matrix
{\mathbf{V}}^{\chi}
.
In Lemma
1
(iv) we prove that (see also (
2
) and (
3
))
for all
j=1,\ldots,r
,

m_{\Lambda}^{2}m_{F}\leq\underline{C}_{j}\!\leq\lim\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{j}^{\chi}}{n}\leq\lim\sup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{j}^{\chi}}{n}\leq\!\overline{C}_{j}\leq M_{\Lambda}^{2}M_{F}.

(4)

This means we consider only pervasive strong factors.

To characterize the idiosyncratic component, we make the following assumptions.

Assumption 2

(
idiosyncratic component
)
.

(a)

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}]=0
and
\sigma_{i}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}^{2}]\geq C_{\xi}
for some finite positive real
C_{\xi}
for some finite positive real
C_{\xi}
independent of
i
and
t
.

(b)

For all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{j,t-k}]|\leq\rho^{|k|}M_{ij}
, where
\rho
and
M_{ij}
are finite positive reals independent of
t
such that
0\leq\rho<1
,
\sum_{j=1}^{n}M_{ij}\leq M_{\xi}
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{ij}\leq M_{\xi}
for some finite positive real
M_{\xi}
independent of
i
,
j
, and
n
.

(c)

(i)

For all
i=1,\ldots,n
, all
t=1,\ldots,T
, and all
n,T\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}^{4}]\leq Q_{\xi}
for some finite positive real
\phantom{(i)}\,Q_{\xi}
independent of
i
and
t
;

(ii)

for all
j=1,\ldots,n
, all
s=1,\ldots,T
, and all
n,T\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right|^{2}\right]\leq K_{\xi}

for some finite positive real
K_{\xi}
independent of
j
,
s
,
n
, and
T
.

By part (a), we have that the idiosyncratic components have zero mean. This, jointly with Assumption
1
(b) by which
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{F}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{r}
, implies that we are implicitly assuming that the data has zero mean, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Obviously, we could modify (
1
) to allow for non-zero mean in the data, by just adding a constant term. Since there are no additional difficulties in doing so and all results that follow would still hold once we pre-center the data, we do not consider this case, to keep the notation simple.

Part (b) has a twofold purpose. First, it limits the degree of serial correlation of the idiosyncratic components, implying weak stationarity of idiosyncratic components. Second, it also limits the degree of cross-sectional correlation between idiosyncratic components, which is usually assumed in approximate DFMs. Moreover, part (b) implies the usual conditions required by
Bai 2003
for PC estimation (see Lemma
1
(i)-
1
(iii)), i.e.,

\displaystyle\sup_{n,T\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}|\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{js}]|\leq\frac{M_{\xi}(1+\rho)}{1-\rho},

\displaystyle\sup_{n,T\in\mathbb{N}}\max_{t=1,\ldots,T}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}|\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]|\leq M_{\xi},

(5)

\displaystyle\sup_{n,T\in\mathbb{N}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,n}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}|\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{is}]|\leq\frac{M_{\xi}(1+\rho)}{1-\rho},

where
M_{\xi}
and
\rho
are defined in Assumption
2
(b). In fact
Bai 2003
directly assumes conditions as those in (
5
) without asking for stationarity.
Notice that the formulation of part (b) is inspired by
Forni
et al. 2017
and it implicitly bounds the L1 norm of
\bm{\Gamma}^{\xi}
as in
Fan
et al. 2013
.
Furthermore in Lemma
1
(v) we prove that the largest eigenvalue of
\bm{\Gamma}^{\xi}
is such that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{1}^{\xi}\leq M_{\xi},

(6)

where
M_{\xi}
is defined in Assumption
2
(b). Condition (
6
) was originally assumed by
Chamberlain and
Rothschild 1983
. Finally, by setting
k=0
in Assumption
2
(b), it follows also that for all
i=1,\ldots,N
and all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sigma_{i}^{2}\leq M_{\xi}
. Thus, all idiosyncratic components have finite variance.

Part (c-i) assumes finite 4th order moments of the idiosyncratic components, it is weaker than what assumed by
Bai 2003
where finite 8th order moments are required. Part (c-ii) gives summability conditions across the cross-section and time dimensions for the 4th order cumulants of
\{\xi_{it}\}
, indeed, it can be equivalently written as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right)\left(\sum_{u=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{ks}\xi_{ju}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{ks}\xi_{ju}]\right\}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}\sum_{t,u=1}^{T}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}\xi_{ks}\xi_{ju}\right]-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}]\mathbb{E}[\xi_{ks}\xi_{ju}]\right\}\leq K_{\xi}.

Then, if we choose
s=t
we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right|^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}\sum_{t,u=1}^{T}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}\xi_{ku}\xi_{ju}\right]-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]\mathbb{E}[\xi_{ku}\xi_{ju}]\right\}\leq K_{\xi},

which is assumed by
Bai and Li 2016
. And if we choose
j=i
we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{it}\xi_{is}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{is}]\right\}\right|^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{t,u=1}^{T}\sum_{i,k=1}^{n}\left\{\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{is}\xi_{ku}\xi_{ks}]-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{is}]\mathbb{E}[\xi_{ku}\xi_{ks}]\right\}\leq K_{\xi},

which is similar to what is assumed by
Bai 2003
, where, however,
T=1
, and 8th cross-moments are bounded.

Part (c-ii) has some important consequences. First, it follows that (see Lemma
3
(iii) and (
A81
) in the proof of Proposition
6
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{n\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\xi}_{t}\bm{\xi}_{t}^{\prime}-\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[\bm{\xi}_{t}\bm{\xi}_{t}^{\prime}]\right\}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]\leq rM_{\Lambda}K_{\xi},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}T}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\bm{\zeta}_{i}\bm{\zeta}_{i}^{\prime}\bm{F}-\mathbb{E}[\bm{\zeta}_{i}\bm{\zeta}_{i}^{\prime}]\bm{F}\right\}\right\|_{F}^{2}\right]\leq rM_{F}K_{\xi},

where the second inequality is analogous to
Bai 2003
. Second, part (c-ii) implies also that (setting
n=1
therein):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{u=1}^{T}\left\{\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}\xi_{is}\xi_{ju}]-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{jt}]\mathbb{E}[\xi_{is}\xi_{ju}]\right\}\leq\frac{K_{\xi}}{T},

which means that the sample (auto)covariances between
\{\xi_{it}\}
and
\{\xi_{jt}\}
are
\sqrt{T}
-consistent estimators of their population counterparts. In particular, choosing
s=t
, we see that we can consistently estimate the
(i,j)
th entry of the idiosyncratic covariance matrix
\bm{\Gamma}^{\xi}
.
Again this assumption requires introducing the sample size
T
and it is stronger that what we ask for the factors in Assumption
1
(c), which must hold only in the limit
T\to\infty
, while here it holds for all
T\in\mathbb{N}
.
As for the factors, a discussion on the primitive conditions the process
\{\xi_{it}\}
needs to satisfy is postponed in Section
3
where alternative approaches to directly assuming part (c) are proposed.

We conclude with two identifying assumptions.

Assumption 3

(
distinct eigenvalues
)
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
i=1,\ldots,r-1
,
\mu_{i}^{\chi}>\mu_{i+1}^{\chi}
.

Note that this implies that
\overline{C}_{j}<\underline{C}_{j-1}
,
j=2,\ldots,r
, in (
4
) and that the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{F}
are distinct, as also required in
Bai 2003
. Furthermore, letting
\bm{V}_{0}
be the
r\times r
diagonal matrix of non-zero eigenvalues of

(\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda})^{1/2}\bm{\Gamma}^{F}(\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda})^{1/2}
or equivalently of

(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})^{1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})^{1/2}
, it follows that these are also distinct because of Assumption
3
.
1
1

1

In Lemma
8
we also show that the diagonal entries
V_{0,jj}
,
j=1,\ldots,r
, of
\bm{V}_{0}
are such that
V_{0,jj}=\mathrm{p}\text{-}\!\lim_{n,T\to\infty}\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\right)=\mathrm{p}\text{-}\!\lim_{n,T\to\infty}\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}\right)
.

This is needed in order to identify the eigenvectors when doing PCA.

Assumption 4

(
independence
)
.

The processes

\{\xi_{it},\,i\in\mathbb{N},\,t\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and
\{F_{jt},\,j=1,\ldots,r,\,t\in\mathbb{Z}\}
are mutually independent.

This assumption obviously implies that the factors and, therefore, the common components are independent of the idiosyncratic components at all leads and lags and across all units. This is compatible with a structural macroeconomic interpretation of factor models, according to which the factors driving the common component are independent of the idiosyncratic components representing measurement errors or local dynamics. Assumption
4
implies (see the proof of Lemma
3
(i))

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\bm{\xi}_{t}^{\prime}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq M_{F\xi},

for some finite positive real
M_{F\xi}
independent of
n
and
T
,
and also (see (
A16
), (
A22
) and (
A23
) in the proof of Proposition
2
)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq M_{F\xi},\hskip 20.00003pt\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{jt}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq M_{F\xi},

which are similar but weaker than what assumed by
Bai 2003
, i.e.,

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{jt}\right\|^{2}\right]\leq M_{F\xi}.

(7)

Notice that all instances where we use Assumption
4
would follow if we used (
7
) directly.
Note that if we assumed random loadings then here we should add that also
\{\lambda_{ij},\,i\in\mathbb{N},\,j=1,\ldots,r\}
is independent of the factor and idiosyncratic processes.

Because of Weyl’s inequality (
6
) and (
4
) and Assumption
4
imply the eigengap in the eigenvalues
\mu_{j}^{x}
,
j=1,\ldots,n
, of
\bm{\Gamma}^{x}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{x}_{t}\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\prime}]=\bm{\Gamma}^{\chi}+\bm{\Gamma}^{\xi}
(see Lemma
1
(vi)):

\displaystyle\underline{C}_{r}\leq\lim\inf_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{r}^{x}}{n}\leq\lim\sup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{r}^{x}}{n}\leq\overline{C}_{r},

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mu_{r+1}^{x}\leq M_{\xi},

where
M_{\xi}
is defined in Assumption
2
(b).

Assumption 5

(
Central limit theorems
)
.

(a)

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, as
T\to\infty
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0}_{r},\bm{\Phi}_{i}\right),

where
\bm{\Phi}_{i}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{F}_{s}^{\prime}\xi_{it}\xi_{is}]
.

(b)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, as
n\to\infty
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\xi_{it}\to_{d}\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0}_{r},\bm{\Gamma}_{t}\right),

where
\bm{\Gamma}_{t}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]
.

(c)

\sqrt{T}/n\to 0
and
\sqrt{n}/T\to 0
, as
n,T\to\infty
.

Part (a) can be derived from more primitive assumptions. For example, we could assume strong mixing factors and idiosyncratic components and such that, for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we strengthen Assumptions
1
(c-i) and
2
(c-i) to:
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{F}_{t}\|^{4+\epsilon}]\leq K_{F}
and
\mathbb{E}[|\xi_{it}|^{4+\epsilon}]\leq Q_{\xi}
, for some
\epsilon>0
.
Then,
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\}
is also strong mixing because of
Bradley 2005
and with finite 4th order moments because of Assumption
4
and part (a) would follow from
Ibragimov 1962
. For other assumptions see also Section
3
. Notice also that under Assumption
4
in part (a) we actually have
\bm{\Phi}_{i}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{F}_{s}^{\prime}]\,\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{is}]
.

Part (b) of course holds if we assumed cross-sectionally uncorrelated idiosyncratic components, i.e., if
\bm{\Gamma}^{\xi}
were diagonal as in an exact factor model. In general, to derive part (b) from primitive assumptions we could either introduce ordering of the
n
cross-sectional items and a related notion of spatial dependence, or we could derived it from the properties of stationary mixing random fields
(
Bolthausen 1982
)
or of cross-sectional martingale difference sequences
(
Kuersteiner and
Prucha 2013
)
, or we could apply results on exchangeable sequences, which are instead independent of the ordering, and are in turn obtained by virtue of the Hewitt-Savage-de Finetti theorem (
Austern and
Orbanz 2022
, Theorem 4
, and
Bolthausen 1984
). In any case we would need also to strengthen Assumption
2
(c-i) by asking that for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[|\xi_{it}|^{4+\epsilon}]\leq Q_{\xi}
, for some
\epsilon>0
. In the case of random loadings we should also require that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\lambda}_{i}\|^{4+\epsilon}]\leq K_{\Lambda}
, for some
\epsilon>0
, and we would have:

\bm{\Gamma}_{t}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\bm{\lambda}_{i}\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}]\,\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]
.

2
Estimation by means of Principal Component Analysis

All estimated quantities are denoted with
\,\widehat{}\,
or
\,\widetilde{}\,
and depend implicitly on
n
and
T
.

Consider the
n\times n
sample covariance matrix (note that data has zero mean by assumption)

\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{t}\mathbf{x}_{t}^{\prime}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{T},

with
r
largest eigenvalues in the
r\times r
diagonal matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}
(sorted in descending order) and corresponding normalized eigenvectors as columns of the
n\times r
matrix
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}
.

Consider also the
T\times T
sample covariance matrix (note that data has zero mean by assumption)

\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{x}_{i}\bm{x}_{i}^{\prime}=\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{n},

with
r
largest eigenvalues in the
r\times r
diagonal matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}
(sorted in descending order) and corresponding normalized eigenvectors as columns of the
T\times r
matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x}
.

The PC estimators of
\bm{\Lambda}
and
\bm{F}
are the solutions of the following minimization:

\displaystyle\min_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}},\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(x_{it}-\underline{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\underline{\mathbf{F}}_{t})^{2}

\displaystyle=\min_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}},\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\left(\bm{X}-\underline{\bm{F}}\,\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\right)\left(\bm{X}-\underline{\bm{F}}\,\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\right\}=\min_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}},\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\left(\bm{X}-\underline{\bm{F}}\,\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\left(\bm{X}-\underline{\bm{F}}\,\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\right)\right\}.

(8)

The idea of PCs as the
r
directions of best fit in an
n
-dimensional space is originally due to
Pearson 1901
.
There are four equivalent ways to solve the problem in (
8
).

2.1
Approach A by
Forni
et al. 2009

We solve for
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}
such that
\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}
is diagonal and then solve for
\underline{\bm{F}}
by linear projection. So substitute
\underline{\bm{F}}=\bm{X}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}(\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}
in (
8
) and we have

\displaystyle\min_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}\left(\mathbf{I}_{n}-\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}(\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\right)\bm{X}^{\prime}\right\}

(9)

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\max_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}(\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\right\}=\max_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}\frac{1}{n}\text{tr}\left\{(\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1/2}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{T}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}(\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1/2}\right\}

(10)

Now since by construction each column of
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}(\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1/2}
is normalized (since we assumed
\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}
to be diagonal), then the above maximization must give as maximum the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{T}
divided by
n
, i.e., it must give
\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}
. In other words, our estimator
\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}
must be such that
\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1/2}
is the matrix of normalized eigenvectors corresponding the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}
, i.e., such that:

(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1/2}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1/2}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}

(11)

but also

\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x\prime}\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}

(12)

therefore

\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}=\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}(\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x})^{1/2}

(13)

and

\widehat{\bm{F}}=\bm{X}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}=\bm{X}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}(\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x})^{-1/2}

(14)

which are the normalized PCs of
\bm{X}
and such that
\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{F}}}{T}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
. This is the approach followed in the rest of the paper.
It is the classical and original definition of PC estimators dating back to
Hotelling 1933
(see also
Lawley and
Maxwell 1971
, Chapter 4
,
Mardia
et al. 1979
, Chapter 9.3
,
Jolliffe 2002
, Chapter 7.2
).

2.2
Approach B by
Bai 2003

We solve (
8
) for
\underline{\bm{F}}
such that
\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}}{T}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
and then solve for
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}
by linear projection. So substitute
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}=\bm{X}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}(\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}})^{-1}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}}{T}
in (
8
) and we have

\displaystyle\min_{\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{I}_{T}-\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}\,\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}}{T}\right)\bm{X}\right\},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\max_{\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}\,\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}}{T}\right\}=\max_{\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{T}\text{tr}\left\{\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{n}\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}}{\sqrt{T}}\right\}.

Now since by construction each column of
\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}}{\sqrt{T}}
is normalized (since we assumed
\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}}{T}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
), then the above maximization must give as maximum the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}=\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{n}
divided by
T
, i.e., it must give
\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}
. In other words, our estimator
\widetilde{\bm{F}}
must be such that
\frac{\widetilde{\bm{F}}}{\sqrt{T}}
is the matrix of normalized eigenvectors corresponding the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}
, i.e., such that:

\frac{\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}\frac{\widetilde{\bm{F}}}{\sqrt{T}}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}

but also

\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x\prime}\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}

therefore

\widetilde{\bm{F}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x}\sqrt{T}

and

\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}}}{T}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x}}{\sqrt{T}}

which are such that
\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}
is diagonal.

2.3
Approach C by
Stock and
Watson 2002

We solve (
8
) for
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}
such that
\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
and then solve for
\underline{\bm{F}}
by linear projection. So substitute
\underline{\bm{F}}=\bm{X}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}(\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}=\frac{\bm{X}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}
in (
8
) and we have

\displaystyle\min_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}\left(\mathbf{I}_{n}-\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}\,\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}}{n}\right)\bm{X}^{\prime}\right\},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\max_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}\,\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}}{n}\right\}=\max_{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}\frac{1}{n}\text{tr}\left\{\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{T}\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\}.

Now since by construction each column of
\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}{\sqrt{n}}
is normalized (since we assumed
\frac{\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
), then the above maximization must give as maximum the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}=\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{T}
divided by
n
, i.e., it must give
\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}
. In other words, our estimator
\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}
must be such that
\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{\sqrt{n}}
is the matrix of normalized eigenvectors corresponding the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}
, i.e., such that:

\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{\sqrt{n}}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n},

but also

\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x\prime}\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}.

Therefore,

\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}=\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}\sqrt{n},

and

\widehat{\bm{F}}=\frac{\bm{X}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}=\frac{\bm{X}\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}}{\sqrt{n}},

which are such that
\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{F}}}{T}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}
is diagonal.

2.4
Approach D

We solve for
\underline{\bm{F}}
such that
\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}}{T}
is diagonal and then solve for
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}
by linear projection. So substitute
\underline{\bm{\Lambda}}=\bm{X}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}(\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}})^{-1}
in (
8
) and we have

\displaystyle\min_{\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{I}_{T}-\underline{\bm{F}}(\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}})^{-1}\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\right)\bm{X}\right\}

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\max_{\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{nT}\text{tr}\left\{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}(\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}})^{-1}\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\right\}=\max_{\underline{\bm{F}}}\frac{1}{T}\text{tr}\left\{(\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}})^{-1/2}\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{n}\underline{\bm{F}}(\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}})^{-1/2}\right\}

Now since by construction each column of
\underline{\bm{F}}(\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}})^{-1/2}
is normalized (since we assumed
\frac{\underline{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\underline{\bm{F}}}{T}
to be diagonal), then the above maximization must give as maximum the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\widetilde{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}=\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{n}
divided by
T
, i.e., it must give
\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}
. In other words, our estimator
\widetilde{\bm{F}}
must be such that
\widetilde{\bm{F}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}})^{-1/2}
is the matrix of normalized eigenvectors corresponding the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}
, i.e., such that:

(\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}})^{-1/2}\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}\widetilde{\bm{F}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}})^{-1/2}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}

but also

\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x\prime}\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}

therefore

\widetilde{\bm{F}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x}(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x})^{1/2}

and

\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}=\bm{X}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}}(\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}})^{-1}=\bm{X}^{\prime}\widetilde{\mathbf{V}}^{x}(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x})^{-1/2}

which are the normalized PCs of
\bm{X}^{\prime}
and such that
\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
.

2.5
Comparison of the four approaches

The four methods are asymptotically equivalent. Indeed, as long as
r<\min(n,T)
the
r
largest eigenvalues of
\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}
and those of
\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}
coincide. More precisely, by Weyl’s inequality, in case A and C, for
j=1,\ldots,r
,

\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}{nT}\right)+\mu_{n}\left(\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}}{nT}\right)\leq\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\right)\leq\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}{nT}\right)+\mu_{1}\left(\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}}{nT}\right)

and since, by continuity of eigenvalues and Assumptions
1
(a) and
1
(c),

\lim_{n,T\to\infty}\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}{nT}\right)=\lim_{n,T\to\infty}\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{nT}\right)=\mu_{j}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\right)

and in Lemma
3
we prove
\left\|\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}}{nT}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\right)
, then

\left|\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\right)-\mu_{j}\left(\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\right)\right|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1).

So letting
\bm{V}_{0}
be the
r\times r
diagonal matrix with entries the eigenvalues
\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{F}
, we have proved that in case A

\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=\left\|\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1),

and in case C

\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{F}}}{T}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=\left\|\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1),

Similarly, in case B and D

\left|\mu_{j}\left(\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}\right)-\mu_{j}\left(\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}\right)\right|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1).

So letting
\bm{V}_{0}
be the
r\times r
diagonal matrix with entries the eigenvalues of
\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}
, which are the same as those of
\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}\bm{\Gamma}^{F}
, we have proved that in case B

\left\|\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=\left\|\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1),

and in case D

\left\|\frac{\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}}}{T}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=\left\|\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T}-\bm{V}_{0}\right\|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1).

3
On the estimation of the covariance matrix

To be able to apply PCA we need a consistent estimator of the covariance matrix of
\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\}
. In fact, we just need consistency of the estimators of each entry of the matrix. Indeed, for the scope of factor analysis, we do not need a consistency result for the whole matrix, but we just need to ensure that

\frac{1}{n}\left\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}-\bm{\Gamma}^{x}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(15)

So even if when
n>T
the sample covariance matrix is not positive definite, this does not affect any of the methods presented in the previous section nor the results in the next sections. The proof of (
15
) is in Lemma
5
(i), and it relies on Assumption
1
(c) for estimation of the covariance of the factors and on Assumption
2
(c) for estimation of the covariance of the idiosyncratic components. Both these assumptions are high-level ones and it is natural to ask when are they satisfied.
In this section, we introduce more primitive conditions on the data generating process of
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
and
\{\xi_{it}\}
which ensure that (
15
) holds, or even that:

\frac{1}{n}\left\|\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}-\bm{\Gamma}^{x}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{m}s}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(16)

The proof of (
16
) under the assumptions given below is in Lemma
5
(ii).

3.1
Consistent estimation of the covariance of the factors

We consider four different ways to state primitive conditions such that Assumption
1
(c) holds.

3.1.1
Linear representation

Assume that
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
admits the following linear representation:

\mathbf{F}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{c}_{kj}^{\prime}u_{jt}=\mathbf{C}(L)\mathbf{u}_{t},

(17)

where
\mathbf{C}(L)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{C}_{k}L^{k}
is an
r\times r
matrix of polynomials with coefficients
\mathbf{C}_{k}=(\mathbf{c}_{k1}\cdots\mathbf{c}_{kr})^{\prime}
. We characterize (
17
) by means of the following assumption.

Assumption 6
.

(W1)

\mathbf{C}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{C}_{k}z^{k}
, where
{\mathbf{C}}_{k}
are
r\times r
and
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathbf{C}_{k}\|\leq M_{C}
for some finite positive real
M_{C}
.

(W2)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{u}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{r}
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{u}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\prime}]=\mathbf{I}_{r}
.

(W3)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k\neq 0
,
\mathbf{u}_{t}
is independent of
\mathbf{u}_{t-k}
.

(W4)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{u}_{t}\|^{4}]\leq K_{u}
for some finite positive real
K_{u}
independent of
t
.

Because of Assumption
6
(W1)-
6
(W4) and by using explicitly (
17
), we can prove that (see Lemma
4
(i))

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}-\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\right\}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{ms}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(18)

This is the approach adopted for example by
Barigozzi
et al. 2021
when considering
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
as a cointegrated
I(1)
process and representation (
17
) is assumed for the differenced process
\{\Delta\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
, where then
\mathbf{C}(1)
has reduced rank equal to the number of common trends.

An implicit argument leading to consistency of the sample covariance matrix under Assumption
6
(W1)-
6
(W4) is that
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
is ergodic with finite 4th order moments, therefore
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\}
is also ergodic. However, this would lead to convergence in probability only (see below).

Independence of the innovations in Assumption
6
(W3) might be too much to ask, and, by relaxing it we could allow, for example, for conditional heteroskedasticity in
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
. This can be done by asking that for all
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}=1,\ldots,r
,

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\left|\mathbb{E}[u_{j_{1}t}u_{j_{2}t}u_{j_{3}s}u_{j_{4}s}]]\right|\leq C_{u},\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\left|\mathbb{E}[u_{j_{1}t}u_{j_{2}t}]\mathbb{E}[u_{j_{3}s}u_{j_{4}s}]\right|\leq C_{u},

(19)

for some finite positive real
C_{u}
independent of
t
,
s
,
j_{1}
,
j_{2}
,
j_{3}
, and
j_{4}
.
Notice that the second condition in (
19
) holds trivially if
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
is a white noise process. In this case, however, we should change Assumption
6
(W4) by asking that for all
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}=1,\ldots,r
, all
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}=1,\ldots,T
and all
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[|u_{j_{1}t_{1}}u_{j_{2}t_{2}}u_{j_{3}t_{3}}u_{j_{4}t_{4}}|]\leq K_{u}
for some finite positive real
K_{u}
independent of
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}
,
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
, and
T
. These changes would still ensure that
T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{u}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\prime}
is a consistent estimator of the covariance matrix of
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
(see the arguments in the next section).

3.1.2
Fourth order cumulants

Alternatively, we could directly assume for all
i,j=1,\ldots,r
,

Assumption 7
.

(H1)

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\left|\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{jt}F_{is}F_{js}]\right|\leq C_{F}
;

(H2)

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\left|\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{jt}]\mathbb{E}[F_{is}F_{js}]\right|\leq C_{F}
;

(H3)

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\left|\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{is}]\mathbb{E}[F_{jt}F_{js}]\right|\leq C_{F}
;

(H4)

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\left|\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{js}]\mathbb{E}[F_{is}F_{jt}]\right|\leq C_{F}
;

for some finite positive real
C_{F}
independent of
i
and
j
.

Then, it is straightforward to see that (
18
) holds (see Lemma

4
(i)). Notice that, since
\mathbb{E}[F_{it}]=0
by Assumption
1
(b), then

\text{cum}_{4}(F_{it},F_{jt},F_{is},F_{js})=\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{jt}F_{is}F_{js}]-\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{jt}]\mathbb{E}[F_{is}F_{js}]-\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{is}]\mathbb{E}[F_{jt}F_{js}]-\mathbb{E}[F_{it}F_{js}]\mathbb{E}[F_{jt}F_{is}],

and we are in fact assuming summability of the 4th order cumulants, which is necessary and sufficient condition for (
18
) to hold (
Hannan 1970
, pp. 209-211
). This approach is a classical one and it is high-level in that it does not make any specific assumption on the dynamics of
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
. On this approach see also
Forni
et al. 2009
.

3.1.3
Mixing

Alternatively we can assume that:

(M1)

\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
is strong mixing, i.e.,
\alpha
-mixing, with exponentially decaying mixing coefficients

\alpha(T)\leq\exp(-c_{1}T^{c_{2}})
,
for some finite positive reals
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
independent of
T
.

(M2)

One of the following two:

(M2a)

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathbf{F}_{t}\|^{4}]\leq K_{F}
for some finite positive real
K_{F}
independent of
t
.

(M2b)

For all
s>0
and
j=1,\ldots,r
,
\mathrm{P}(|F_{jt}|>s)\leq\exp(-bs^{k})
for some finite positive reals
b
and
k
independent of
t
and
j
.

Notice that (M2b) is much stronger than (M2a), indeed it is equivalent to the Cramér condition:
\sup_{m\geq 1}r^{-1/k}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left|F_{jt}\right|^{m}\right]\right)^{1/m}\leq K_{F}^{\prime}
, for some finite positive real
K_{F}^{\prime}
independent of t and j (
Kuchibhotla and
Chakrabortty 2020
, Section 2
), which implies (M2a). The approach based on (M1) and (M2b) is considered in
Fan
et al. 2013
.

Then,
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
is ergodic (
White 2001
, Proposition 3.44
, and
Rosenblatt 1972
), and therefore
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\}
is also ergodic (
White 2001
, Theorem 3.35
, and
Stout 1974
, pp. 170, 182
), so that

\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}-\bm{\Gamma}^{F}\right\}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

In the same spirit of (M1)-(M2) we could assume
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
to admit the linear representation (
17
), with the coefficients
\{\mathbf{C}_{k}\}
satisfying Assumption
6
(W1) and
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
satisfying Assumptions
6
(W2)-
6
(W3), plus the integral Lipschitz condition:

(M3)

for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
has pdf
f_{\mathbf{u}_{t}}(\bm{u})
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\left|f_{\mathbf{u}_{t}}(\bm{u}+\bm{v})-f_{\mathbf{u}_{t}}(\bm{v})\right|\leq C_{f}\|\bm{v}\|
, for any
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and for some finite positive real
C_{f}
independent of
t
.

Then, Assumptions
6
(W1)-
6
(W3) and (M3) imply that
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
is strong mixing with exponentially decaying coefficients, i.e., (M1) holds (see
Pham and Tran 1985
, Theorem 3.1
). Note that independence in Assumption
6
(W3) is not strictly necessary for having (M1) to hold, as we could allow for GARCH effects by assuming geometric ergodicity of
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
instead. Indeed, geometric ergodicity implies
\beta
-mixing, which implies strong mixing (
Francq and
Zakoïan 2006
).

3.1.4
Physical or functional dependence

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t}
be the
\sigma
-field generated by
\{\bm{\nu}_{s},\,s\leq t\}
where
\bm{\nu}_{t}=(\nu_{1t}\cdots\nu_{rt})^{\prime}
. Let also
g_{j,u}(\cdot)
be a real-valued measurable function of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t}
and define
\mathbf{G}_{u}(\cdot)=(g_{1,u}(\cdot)\cdots g_{r,u}(\cdot))^{\prime}
. Then, we assume that
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
admits the following representation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{c}_{kj}^{\prime}u_{jt}=\mathbf{C}(L)\mathbf{u}_{t},

(20)

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{G}_{u}(\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t}).

(21)

For any
\ell=1,\ldots,r
and
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, define the element-wise physical or functional dependence measure as

\delta_{t,d,\ell}^{u}=\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g_{\ell,u}(\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t})-g_{\ell,u}(\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t,\{0\}})\right\|^{d}\right]\right\}^{1/d},

where
\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t,\{0\}}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\{\bm{\nu}_{t},\ldots,\bm{\nu}_{1},\bm{\nu}_{0}^{*},\bm{\nu}_{-1},\ldots\}
where
\bm{\nu}_{0}^{*}
is an i.i.d. copy of
\bm{\nu}_{0}
.
Finally, define the uniform dependence adjusted norm as:

\Phi_{d,\alpha}^{u}=\max_{\ell=1,\ldots,r}\sup_{k\geq 0}(1+k)^{\alpha}\sum_{t=k}^{\infty}\delta_{t,d,\ell}^{u}.

(22)

We characterize (
20
)-(
21
) by means of the following assumption.

Assumption 8
.

(P1)

\mathbf{C}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{C}_{k}z^{k}
, where
{\mathbf{C}}_{k}
are
r\times r
and
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathbf{C}_{k}\|\leq M_{C}
for some finite positive real
M_{C}
.

(P2)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\nu}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{r}
,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\nu}_{t}\bm{\nu}_{t}^{\prime}]=\mathbf{I}_{r}
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{u}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{r}
, and
\bm{\Gamma}^{u}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{u}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\prime}]
is
r\times r
positive definite and such that
\|\bm{\Gamma}^{u}\|\leq M_{u}
for some finite positive real
M_{u}
independent of
t
.

(P3)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k\neq 0
,
\bm{\nu}_{t}
is independent of
\bm{\nu}_{t-k}
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{u}_{t}\mathbf{u}_{t-k}^{\prime}]=\mathbf{0}_{r\times r}
.

(P4)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
\ell=1,\ldots,r
,
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{g_{\ell,u}(\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t})\right\}^{q}\right]\leq K_{u}
for some
q\geq 4
and some finite positive real
K_{u}
independent of
\ell
and
t
.

(P5)

For all
d\leq q
with
q>4
,
\Phi_{d,\alpha}^{u}\leq M_{u,d}
for some finite positive real
M_{u,d}
independent of
\alpha
.

We can then prove that, under Assumption
8
, (
18
) still holds (see Lemma
4
(i)).

Parts (P1)-(P3) are simply defining the Wold representation of
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
as being driven by a weak white noise
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
which in turn can be a function of an i.i.d. process
\{\bm{\nu}_{t}\}
. Moreover, from (P4) and (P5) it follows that we must have
\mathbb{E}[|u_{\ell t}|^{4+\epsilon}]\leq K_{u}
which is slightly stringer than Assumption
6
(W4).

Finally (P5) deserves two comments. First, as seen from the definition of physical dependence (
22
), for (P5) to hold we must choose an appropriate value of
\alpha
, which, in principle, is constrained by the degree of serial dependence in
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
. Now, since in (P3) we make the natural assumption of
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
being a weak white noise, then no constraint on
\alpha
is needed for (P5) to hold. And the same is true if we assumed a geometric decay of the lag-
k
autocovariance of
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
, i.e.,
\rho^{k}
for some
0<\rho<1
. In general, we could even assume an hyperbolic decay of the lag-
k
autocovariance of
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
, i.e., as
(1+k)^{-\varsigma}
, with
\varsigma>2
and in that case (P5) would hold as long as we choose
\alpha\leq\varsigma-1
(see
Barigozzi
et al. 2022
, Lemma C.1
). In all cases, since
q>4
, we can choose
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{q}
, which is the condition needed to apply the results of
Zhang and Wu 2021
.

Second, part (P5) is very general. It includes non linear models as threshold autoregressive models, exponential autoregressive models, and conditionally heteroskedastic models, e.g., univariate stationary GARCH(1,1) models, i.e.,when
g_{\ell,u}(\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t})=(\omega_{\ell}+\alpha_{\ell}u_{\ell,t-1}^{2}+\beta_{\ell}\sigma_{\ell,t-1}^{2})^{1/2}\nu_{\ell t}
with
\omega_{\ell}>0
,
\alpha_{\ell},\beta_{\ell}\geq 0
and
\alpha_{\ell}+\beta_{\ell}<1
for all
\ell=1,\ldots,r
. For all these cases, it is shown in
Wu 2005
that Assumption
8
(P5) is satisfied. Obviously, the linear representation in (
17
) is a special case of (
20
)-(
21
) when
\bm{\nu}_{t}\equiv\mathbf{u}_{t}
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and
g_{\ell,u}(\mathcal{F}_{\nu,t})=u_{\ell t}
for all
\ell=1,\ldots,r
. Then, it is easy to see that Assumption
8
(P5) holds if we make Assumption
6
(W4), i.e., finite 4th moments of innovations. This can be shown using the same arguments in
Forni
et al. 2017
but applied to
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
.

3.2
Consistent estimation of the covariance of the idiosyncratic components

Assumption
2
(c) already implies, as a special case, a condition on summability of 4th order moments along the time dimension as assumed in Assumption
7
for the factors. Hence, it immediately follows that, for any
i,j=1,\ldots,n
(see Lemma
4
(ii))

\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right|=O_{\mathrm{ms}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(23)

Analogously to what discussed for the factors, in place of Assumption
2
(c) we could make more primitive assumptions.

First, we could assume a linear representation:

\xi_{it}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{k,ij}\eta_{j,t-k}.

Then, letting
\bm{\eta}_{t}=(\eta_{1t}\cdots\eta_{nt})^{\prime}
, we can assume the analogous of Assumptions
6
(W1)-
6
(W4).

(WI1)

For all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
,
|\beta_{k,ij}|\leq\rho^{k}B_{ij}
with
0\leq\rho<1
,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{ij}\leq B
and
\sum_{j=1}^{n}B_{ij}\leq B
for some finite positive real
B
independent of
i
and
j
.

(WI2)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{n}
,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}_{t}\bm{\eta}_{t}^{\prime}]=\mathbf{I}_{n}
.

(WI3)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k\neq 0
,
\bm{\eta}_{t}
is independent of
\bm{\eta}_{t-k}
.

(WI4)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[|\eta_{jt}|^{4}]\leq K_{\eta}
for some finite positive real
K_{\eta}
independent of
t
and
j
.

Under (WI1)-(WI4) we can still prove (
23
) (see, e.g., Lemma 11 in
Forni
et al. 2017
for a similar setting and proof).

Second, we could assume
\{\xi_{it}\}
to be strong mixing, i.e.,
\alpha
-mixing, with exponentially decaying mixing coefficients and that
\mathbb{E}[|\xi_{it}|^{4}]\leq K_{\xi}
for some finite positive real
K_{\xi}
independent of
t
and
i
. Or we could even assume exponentially decaying tails as in (M2b). This would imply ergodicity for
\{\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}\}
, and, therefore,

\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right|=O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

This latter approach is in
Fan
et al. 2013
.

Third, we could instead introduce an
n
-dimensional process
\{\bm{\omega}_{t}\}
such that
\{\xi_{it}\}
follows the representation:

\displaystyle\xi_{it}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\beta_{k,ij}\eta_{j,t-k},

\displaystyle\eta_{jt}

\displaystyle=g_{j,\eta}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega,t}),

where
\mathcal{F}_{\omega,t}
is the
\sigma
-field generated by
\{\bm{\omega}_{s},\,s\leq t\}
, and
g_{j,\eta}(\cdot)
is a real-valued measurable function of
\mathcal{F}_{\omega,t}
. Define the uniform dependence adjusted norm as:

\Phi_{d,\alpha}^{\eta}=\max_{\ell=1,\ldots,n}\sup_{k\geq 0}(1+k)^{\alpha}\sum_{t=k}^{\infty}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|g_{\ell,\eta}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega,t})-g_{\ell,\eta}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega,t,\{0\}})\right\|^{d}\right]\right\}^{1/d},

where
\mathcal{F}_{\omega,t,\{0\}}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\{\bm{\omega}_{t},\ldots,\bm{\omega}_{1},\bm{\omega}_{0}^{*},\bm{\omega}_{-1},\ldots\}
where
\bm{\omega}_{0}^{*}
is an i.i.d. copy of
\bm{\omega}_{0}
. Then, we could assume the analogous of Assumption
8
(P1)-
8
(P5).

(PI1)

For all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, all
i,j=1,\ldots,n
, and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
|\beta_{k,ij}|\leq\rho^{k}B_{ij}
with
0\leq\rho<1
,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{ij}\leq B
and
\sum_{j=1}^{n}B_{ij}\leq B
for some finite positive real
B
independent of
i
and
j
.

(PI2)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\omega}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{n}
,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\omega}_{t}\bm{\omega}_{t}^{\prime}]=\mathbf{I}_{n}
,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}_{t}]=\mathbf{0}_{n}
, and
\bm{\Gamma}^{\eta}=\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}_{t}\bm{\eta}_{t}^{\prime}]
is positive definite and such that
\|\bm{\Gamma}^{\eta}\|\leq M_{\eta}
for some finite positive real
M_{\eta}
independent of
t
.

(PI3)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k\neq 0
,
\bm{\omega}_{t}
is independent of
\bm{\omega}_{t-k}
and
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\eta}_{t}\bm{\eta}_{t-k}^{\prime}]=\mathbf{0}_{n\times n}
.

(PI4)

For all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, all
j=1,\ldots,n
, and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[\left\{g_{j,\eta}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega,t})\right\}^{q}]\leq K_{\eta}
for some
q\geq 4
and some finite positive real
K_{\eta}
independent of
t
and
j
.

(PI5)

For all
d\leq q
with
q>4
,
\Phi_{d,\alpha}^{\eta}\leq M_{\eta,d}
for some finite positive real
M_{\eta,d}
independent of
\alpha
.

Under (PI1)-(PI5) we can prove that (
23
) still holds. The same comments made for Assumption
8
(P5) in the case of the factors, apply also here, in particular notice that (PI4)-(PI5) imply
\mathbb{E}[|\eta_{jt}|^{4+\epsilon}]\leq K_{\eta}
which is slightly stronger than with (WI4).

3.3
Consistent estimation of the covariance between factors and idiosyncratic components

From Assumption
4
it follows that for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
, all
i=1,\ldots n
, and all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}]=\mathbf{0}_{r}
. Moreover, as a consequence of Assumption
5
(a) we have:

\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right|=O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(24)

It is then natural to ask if we can find primitive assumptions such that (
24
) or even Assumption
5
(a) holds.

Now, if we assume that one of the following holds:

(C1)

Assumption
6
(W1)-
6
(W4) and (WI1)-(WI4) hold with
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
and
\{\bm{\eta}_{t}\}
independent.

(C2)

Assumption
8
(P1)-
8
(P5) and (PI1)-(PI5) hold with
\{\bm{\nu}_{t}\}
and
\{\bm{\omega}_{t}\}
independent.

Then it is immediate to show that under (C1) (
24
) holds, even in mean-square, that is

\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right|=O_{\mathrm{ms}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(25)

Under (C2) the same holds, just notice that because
\{\bm{\nu}_{t}\}
and
\{\bm{\omega}_{t}\}
are independent then also
\{\mathbf{u}_{t}\}
and
\{\bm{\eta}_{t}\}
are independent and the uniform dependence adjusted norm of the process
\{(\mathbf{u}_{t}^{\prime},\bm{\eta}_{t}^{\prime})^{\prime}\}
is such that
\Phi_{d,\alpha}^{u\eta}\leq\Phi_{d,\alpha}^{u}\Phi_{d,\alpha}^{\eta}\leq M_{u,d}M_{\eta,d}
. Then, the results by
Zhang and Wu 2021
can be applied to the estimation of the covariance of the process
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\}
.

If we assume that
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\}
and
\{\xi_{it}\}
are both strongly mixing with exponentially decaying coefficients and have exponentially decaying tails, then
\{\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\}
is also strongly mixing (see, e.g.,
Bradley 2005
, Theorem 5.1.a
) with exponentially decaying coefficients and has exponentially decaying tails (see, e.g.,
Fan
et al. 2011
, Lemma A2
), and by
Ibragimov 1962
the CLT for strongly mixing processes as in Assumption
5
(a) holds and so (
24
) holds.

4
Consistency of loadings and factors space

The following result provides preliminary bounds derived using only properties of sample covariance and its eigenvectors.
It is based on
Fan
et al. 2013
and used for example in
Barigozzi
et al. 2018
;
Barigozzi
et al. 2021
. Part (a) is the only one we need for the subsequent results and it is tighter than what
Bai 2003
and
Bai and Ng 2020
get.

Proposition 1
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
4
, as
n,T\to\infty

(a)

\min(n,\sqrt{T})\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\mathcal{H}}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(b)

{\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})}\left\|\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}-\bm{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\bm{\mathcal{H}}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
, uniformly in
i
;

(c)

\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}-\bm{F}(\bm{\mathcal{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}}{\sqrt{T}}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(d)

{\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})}\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\bm{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
, uniformly in
t
;

where
\bm{\mathcal{H}}=(\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda})^{-1}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\mathbf{V}^{\chi}(\mathbf{M}^{\chi})^{1/2}\mathbf{J}

and
\mathbf{J}
is an
r\times r
diagonal matrix with entries
\pm 1
.

Notice that, as
n\to\infty
,
\bm{\mathcal{H}}
is finite and positive definite because of Lemma
10
, thus
\bm{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}
is well defined.
2
2

2

By looking at the proof of Proposition
1
we see that to prove mean-squared consistency we would need to prove results as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}-{\mathbf{V}}^{\chi}\mathbf{J}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\{\left(\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}\right)^{1/2}-\left(\mathbf{M}^{\chi}\right)^{1/2}\right\}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}-{\mathbf{V}}^{\chi}\mathbf{J}\right)\right\|^{4}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\left\{\left(\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}\right)^{1/2}-\left(\mathbf{M}^{\chi}\right)^{1/2}\right\}\right)\right\|^{4}\right]}=o(1),

because of Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. This would follow if we could show in Lemma
5
that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\frac{1}{n}\left(\widehat{\bm{\Gamma}}^{x}-\bm{\Gamma}^{\chi}\right)\right\|^{4}\right]=o(1),

which requires stronger assumptions as

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\{\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}]\right\}\right|^{4}\right]\leq K_{\xi}

as Assumption C.5 in
Bai 2003
, which amounts to assume finite and summable 8th order cumulants.

5
Asymptotics for the loadings

From (
11
) and (
12
), since by (
13
) we have
\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}=\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{V}}^{x}=\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x})^{-1/2}
which is well defined because of Lemma
7

\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}=\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}.

(26)

Then, substituting
\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}=(\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}+\bm{\Xi}^{\prime})^{\prime}(\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}+\bm{\Xi})
into (
26
)

\displaystyle\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}=\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}.

(27)

Define

\widehat{\mathbf{H}}=\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1},

(28)

which, as
n,T\to\infty
, is finite because of Lemma
11
(i).

Then, from (
27
) and (
28
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}=

\displaystyle\,\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}\right)\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}{\bm{\Lambda}}}{nT}\right)\bm{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}{nT}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}^{\prime}\bm{\Xi}}{nT}\right)(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-\bm{\Lambda}\bm{\mathcal{H}})\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}.

(29)

Taking the
i
th row of (
29
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}=

\displaystyle\,\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{jt}{\bm{\lambda}}_{j}^{\prime}}_{\text{(1.a)}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\xi_{it}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{j}\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}}_{\text{(1.b)}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}}_{\text{(1.c)}}\right)\bm{\mathcal{H}}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle+\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{jt}(\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{j}^{\prime}-\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}\bm{\mathcal{H}})}_{\text{(1.d)}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\xi_{it}\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{j}(\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{j}^{\prime}-\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}\bm{\mathcal{H}})}_{\text{(1.e)}}\right.

\displaystyle\;\;\left.+\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{it}\xi_{jt}(\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{j}^{\prime}-\bm{\lambda}_{j}^{\prime}\bm{\mathcal{H}})}_{\text{(1.f)}}\right)\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}.

(30)

Proposition 2
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
4
, as
n,T\to\infty

(a)

\sqrt{nT}\left\|\text{(1.a)}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(b)

\sqrt{T}\left\|\text{(1.b)}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(c)

\min(n,\sqrt{nT})\left\|\text{(1.c)}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(d)

\min(\sqrt{nT},T)\left\|\text{(1.d)}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(e)

\min(\sqrt{nT},T)\left\|\text{(1.e)}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(f)

\min(n,\sqrt{nT},T)\left\|\text{(1.f)}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

uniformly in
i
.

From (
30
) and Proposition
2
, for any
i=1,\ldots,n
, we get

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}=\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}\bm{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\max\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\right)\right).

(31)

Consistency follows immediately since the first term in (
31
) is
O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)
.

Proposition 3

(Consistency of loadings)
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
4
, as
n,T\to\infty

(a)

\min(n,\sqrt{nT},\sqrt{T})\left\|\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
,
uniformly in
i
;

(b)

\min(n,\sqrt{nT},\sqrt{T})\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(c)

\min(n,\sqrt{nT},\sqrt{T})\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}-\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}}{n}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
.

Part (a) refines the result in Proposition
1
(b). Note that for part (b)
Bai and Ng 2020
get a rate
\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})
.

Proposition 4
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
4
,

(a)

\left\|\bm{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)-\bm{Q}_{0}\right\|=o(1)
, as
n\to\infty
;

(b)

\left\|\bm{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)-\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\right\|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
, as
n,T\to\infty
;

(c)

\left\|\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)-\bm{Q}_{0}\right\|=o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
, as
n,T\to\infty
;

where

\bm{Q}_{0}=\bm{V}_{0}\bm{\mathcal{J}}_{0}\bm{\Upsilon}_{0}^{\prime}(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})^{-1/2}
, and where

\bm{\mathcal{J}}_{0}
is an
r\times r
diagonal matrix with entries
\pm 1
,
\bm{\Upsilon}_{0}
is the
r\times r
matrix having as columns the normalized eigenvectors of
(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})^{1/2}\bm{\Sigma}_{\Lambda}(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})^{1/2}
, and
\bm{V}_{0}
is the
r\times r
matrix of corresponding eigenvalues sorted in descending order.

Notice that rate is not needed in Proposition
4
. To prove the properties of the loadings it is enough to use statement (a), and then (b) and (c) follow directly from Proposition
1
(a). Still, we prove (c) also more explicitly
by analogy with Proposition 1 in
Bai 2003
(see Section
A.5
). Although such complex proof is not needed here.

Hereafter, let

\bm{V}_{nT}=\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n},

which is the
r\times r
diagonal matrix with as entries the
r
largest non-zero eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}
in decreasing order.

Theorem 1

(Asymptotic Normality of loadings)
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
5
, as
n,T\to\infty
, for any given
i=1,\ldots,n
,

\sqrt{T}(\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i})=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)\to_{d}\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0}_{r},\bm{\Theta}_{i}\right),

where
\bm{\Theta}_{i}=\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}\bm{Q}_{0}\bm{\Phi}_{i}\bm{Q}_{0}^{\prime}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}
.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

If
\sqrt{T}/n\to 0
as
n,T\to\infty
, then, from (
31
),
by Proposition
4
(b):

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}(\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i})

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}\bm{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1).

(32)

The proof follows from Slutsky’s theorem and (
5
), and because of Proposition
4
(c), Lemma
8
(iv) Assumption
5
(a).
\Box

6
Asymptotics for the factors

From (
13
) and (
14
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{F}}

\displaystyle=\bm{X}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}=\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}+\bm{\Xi}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}})^{-1}=\frac{\bm{F}\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}.

(33)

Then,

\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}=\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}+\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}

(34)

and

\bm{\Lambda}=(\bm{\Lambda}-\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})+\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1},

(35)

where

\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}=\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)^{-1},

(36)

which, as
n,T\to\infty
, is finite because of Lemma
11
(ii).

Substituting (
34
) and (
35
) into (
33
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{F}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bm{F}(\bm{\Lambda}-\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}+\bm{F}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\bm{F}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}+\frac{\bm{F}(\bm{\Lambda}-\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}})}{n}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}}{n}\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1},

(37)

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{F}}-\bm{F}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\bm{F}(\bm{\Lambda}-\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}})}{n}+\frac{\bm{\Xi}\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}.

(38)

Taking the
t
th row of (
38
)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}^{\prime}-\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\underbrace{\mathbf{F}_{t}^{\prime}\frac{(\bm{\Lambda}-\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}}_{\text{ (2.a)}}+\underbrace{\bm{\xi}_{t}^{\prime}\frac{(\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}-\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}})}{n}}_{\text{(2.b)}}+\underbrace{\bm{\xi}_{t}^{\prime}\frac{\bm{\Lambda}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}}{n}}_{\text{(2.c)}}\right)\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}

(39)

Proposition 5
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
4
, as
n,T\to\infty

(a)

\min(n,\sqrt{nT},T)\|\text{2.a}\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(b)

\min(n,\sqrt{nT},T)\|\text{2.b}\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(c)

\sqrt{n}\|\text{2.c}\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

uniformly in
t
.

Note that the equivalent of part (a) is in
Bai 2003
where the bound is
O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\max\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{T},\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\right)\right)
which is
O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\max\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{T}\right)\right)
since
\sqrt{nT}=\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})\,\max(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})>[\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})]^{2}
.

From (
39
) and Proposition
5
, for any
t=1,\ldots,T
, we get

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}{\mathbf{F}}_{t}=\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{n}\right)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{T},\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\right).

(40)

Consistency follows immediately since the first term in (
40
) is
O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)
.

Proposition 6

(Consistency of factors)
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
4
, as
n,T\to\infty

(a)

\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{nT},{T})\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
,
uniformly in
t
;

(b)

\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{nT},{T})\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}-{\bm{F}}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}}{\sqrt{T}}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
;

(c)

\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{nT},{T})\left\|\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{F}}}{T}-\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}}{T}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}(1)
.

This refines the result in Proposition
1
(c). Note that
Bai and Ng 2020
get a rate
\min(\sqrt{n},\sqrt{T})
.

Theorem 2

(Asymptotic Normality of factors)
.

Under Assumptions
1
through
5
, as
n,T\to\infty
, for any given
t=1,\ldots,T
,

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}{\mathbf{F}}_{t})=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-2}\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\to_{d}\mathcal{N}\left(\mathbf{0}_{r},\bm{\Pi}_{t}\right),

where
\bm{\Pi}_{t}=(\bm{Q}_{0}^{-1})^{\prime}\bm{\Gamma}_{t}\bm{Q}_{0}^{-1}
.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

If
\sqrt{n}/T\to 0
as
n,T\to\infty
, by substituting in (
40
) the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}
in (
28
)

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}{\mathbf{F}}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-2}\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1).

(41)

The proof follows from Slutsky’s theorem and because of Assumptions
1
(c),
5
(b), Lemma
8
(iv), and recalling from Proposition
4
that
\bm{Q}_{0}=\bm{V}_{0}\bm{\mathcal{J}}_{0}\bm{\Upsilon}_{0}^{\prime}(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})^{-1/2}
and, thus,
\bm{Q}_{0}^{-1}=(\bm{\Gamma}^{F})^{1/2}\bm{\Upsilon}_{0}\bm{\mathcal{J}}_{0}\bm{V}_{0}^{-1}
.
\Box

7
Discussion

7.1
Relation to OLS

Here we derive more intuitive asymptotic expansions which show that PCA is indeed equivalent to OLS.

By using the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}
in (
28
) and of
\widehat{\bm{F}}
in (
14
), and by Proposition
6
(a) and
6
(c), another useful expansion of (
5
) is

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}(\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i})

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from Theorem \ref{th:CLTL}(a)}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from def. of $\widehat{\mathbf{H}}$ in \eqref{eq:acca}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}(\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1})^{\prime}}{T}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny move $\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}$ inside}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{F}}}{T}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from consistency Prop. \ref{prop:F2}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from def. of $\widehat{\bm{F}}$ in \eqref{eq:estF}}

(42)

where, for ease of comparison, in the first line we write again the second line of (
5
) used to derive Theorem
1
. This is OLS when, for a fixed
i
, we regress
x_{it}
onto
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}\mathbf{F}_{t}
or, equivalently, onto
\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}
, which is normalized such that
\frac{\widehat{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{F}}}{T}=\mathbf{I}_{r}
.

By using the definition of
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}
in (
28
) and of
\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}
in (
13
), and by Proposition
3
(a) and
3
(c), another useful expansion of (
6
) is

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}{\mathbf{F}}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-2}\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from Theorem \ref{th:CLTF}(a)}

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\lambda}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from def. of $\widehat{\mathbf{H}}$ in \eqref{eq:acca}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from def. of $\widehat{\bm{\Lambda}}$ in \eqref{eq:estL}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}{\bm{\Lambda}}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}}{n}\right)^{-1}\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny from consistency Prop. \ref{prop:L2}}

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}\left(\frac{{\bm{\Lambda}}^{\prime}{\bm{\Lambda}}}{n}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{\tiny move $\widehat{\mathbf{H}}$ outside},

(43)

which is OLS when, for a fixed
t
, we regress
x_{it}
onto
\widehat{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\bm{\lambda}_{i}
or, equivalently, onto
\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}
.

7.2
Comparison with Approach B by
Bai 2003

Define

\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}=\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\bm{F}^{\prime}\widetilde{\bm{F}}}{T}\right)\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}.

(44)

where

\bm{V}_{nT}=\frac{\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{x}}{T},

which is the
r\times r
diagonal matrix with as entries the
r
largest non-zero eigenvalues of
\frac{\bm{X}\bm{X}^{\prime}}{nT}
in decreasing order, which coincide with those of
\frac{\bm{X}^{\prime}\bm{X}}{nT}
.

Now, it is shown that

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{F}}_{t}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\max\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\frac{1}{T}\right)\right),

(45)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}-\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}^{-1}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}\right\|=O_{\mathrm{P}}\left(\max\left(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)\right).

(46)

Theorem 1 in
Bai 2003
for the factors says,

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{F}}_{t})

\displaystyle=\bm{V}_{nT}^{-1}\left(\frac{\widetilde{\bm{F}}^{\prime}\bm{F}}{T}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1).

This is the only expression reported by
Bai 2003
and it is the analogous of the expression we derived for the loadings in the first line of (
7.1
).
Using the definition of
\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}
in (
44
) we also have

\sqrt{n}(\widehat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}-\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}{\mathbf{F}}_{t})=\widetilde{\mathbf{H}}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\bm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\bm{\Lambda}}{n}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}\xi_{it}\right)+o_{\mathrm{P}}(1).

(47)
